2026 OPINION # 1 
 
Date: February 12, 2026 
 
Question Presented: 
 
Can a political committee accept cryptocurrency, such as Bitcoin, as a contribution?  
 
Background: 
 
Cryptocurrency and Bitcoin 
 
A cryptocurrency1 is an electronically based and mathematically created currency;2 that is, a 
digital representation of value that functions as a medium of exchange, a unit of account, and/or 
a store of value.3 The most widely known cryptocurrency, Bitcoin, was invented by the 
pseudonymous Satoshi Nakamoto following the publication of his essay4 titled “Bitcoin: A Peerto-Peer Electronic Cash System.”5 However, there are currently over 9,000 cryptocurrencies with 
circulating supply, meaning they are available to be bought and sold through open exchanges.6 
 
Unlike other payment systems that typically require the involvement of at least one financial 
institution, cryptocurrencies are usually described as "peer-to-peer" – that is, they allow direct 
payments from one person to another without an intermediary. Bitcoin, for example, uses an 
encrypted network to verify and process each transaction.7 Each individual in the network has a 
unique digital wallet, similar to an account, which holds bitcoins. These digital wallets may be 
stored locally on an individual's own computer or on third-party servers.8   
 
A user transfers bitcoin from the user’s online bitcoin wallet either to other users, to merchants 
who accept bitcoin as payment, or through “[t]hird-party exchanges [that] allow bitcoin users to 
exchange their bitcoins back to government-issued currencies.”9 However, the vast majority of 
 
1 Cryptocurrency is commonly referred to as “virtual currency” in New York State regulations. 23 NYCRR Part 
200.2(p) defines “virtual currency” as “any type of digital unit that is used as a medium of exchange or a form of 
digitally stored value. virtual currency shall be broadly construed to include digital units of exchange that: have a 
centralized repository or administrator; are decentralized and have no centralized repository or administrator; or may 
be created or obtained by computing or manufacturing effort.” 
2 Chino v New York Dept. of Fin. Services, 58 Misc 3d 1203(A) [New York Sup Ct 2017]. 
3 I.R.S. Notice 2014-21, IRS Virtual Currency Guidance (2014). 
4 Chino v New York Dept. of Fin. Services, 58 Misc 3d 1203(A) [New York Sup Ct 2017]. 
5 Satoshi Nakamoto, Bitcoin: A Peer-to-Peer Electronic Cash System, Bitcoin 1 (Mar. 23, 2017), 
http://bitcoin.org/bitcoin.pdf [https://perma.cc/8DGC-THBR] 
6 https://coinmarketcap.com/all/views/all. 
7 Julie Andersen Hill, Virtual Currencies & Federal Law, 18 J Consumer & Com L 65, 66 [2014]. 
8 Michael Sherlock, Bitcoin: The Case Against Strict Regulation, 36 Rev Banking & Fin L 975, 979 [2017]. 
9 Fed. Reserve of Chicago, Chicago Fed Letter 317, Bitcoin: A Primer 2 (Dec. 2013),  available at 
http://www.chicagofed.org/digital_assets/publications/chicago_fed_letter/2013/cfldecember2013_317.pdf   
 
2 
bitcoin is either held as a long-term investment or traded through exchanges. A 2020 article 
states that as much as 79% of the current bitcoin market meets one of these two criteria.10 
 
Bitcoin's core developers further explain cryptocurrency transactions: 
 
Behind the scenes, the Bitcoin network is sharing a public ledger 
called the “block chain”. This ledger contains every transaction 
ever processed, allowing a user's computer to verify the validity of 
each transaction. The authenticity of each transaction i s protected 
by digital signatures corresponding to the sending addresses, 
allowing all users to have full control over sending bitcoins from 
their own Bitcoin addresses. In addition, anyone can process 
transactions using the computing power of specialized hardware 
and earn a reward in bitcoins for this service. This is often called 
“mining”.11 
 
Bitcoin is often described as an anonymous payment system, but it is more accurately described 
as a pseudonymous payment system.12 A user does not need to use his or her real identity to pay 
in bitcoin. However, there are ways to potentially identify bitcoin users who do not take steps to 
ensure their anonymity.13 For example, if a user makes his or her bitcoin address public or 
repeatedly uses the same address, it is possible to discover his or her identity. However, certain 
cryptocurrencies are designed to provide greater anonymity than does bitcoin.14 
 
Engaging in virtual currency business activity in New York (such as, for example, providing 
cryptocurrency exchange services) generally requires one or more forms of authorization from 
the New York State Department of Financial Services, such as a Bitlicense or a limited purpose 
trust company charter (as well as, in some cases, a money transmission license under New York 
Banking Law Article 13-B).15 
 
 
10 60% of Bitcoin is Held Long Term as Digital Gold. What About the Rest? , Chainalysis.com, 
https://www.chainalysis.com/blog/bitcoin-market-data-exchanges-trading/ (last visited February 12, 2026). 
11 Frequently Asked Questions: How Does Bitcoin Work?, Bitcoin.org, https://bitcoin.org/en/faq#how-does-bitcoinwork (last visited February 12, 2026). 
12 See Hill at note 9; see also Ivan Pustogarov, Deanonymisation techniques for Tor and Bitcoin, 2015, PHd 
dissertation, http://orbilu.uni.lu/handle/10993/21798  
13 Narayanan, Arvind, et al. Bitcoin and cryptocurrency technologies: a comprehensive introduction. Princeton 
University Press, 2016. 
14 https://www.coindesk.com/markets/2016/11/27/what-investors-should-know-before-trading-zcash (noting that 
developers have created privacy-oriented digital currencies such as Dash, Monero and Zcash, which use 
technologies to help increase the chances of their users remaining anonymous.)   
15 Some virtual currency businesses are licensed in New York under 23 NYCRR Part 200 , available at 
https://www.dfs.ny.gov/virtual_currency_businesses. Others are chartered under the New York Banking Law. See 
e.g. “NYDFS Grants Charter to ‘Gemini’ Bitcoin Exchange Founded by Cameron and Tyler Winklevoss," October 
5, 2015 at https://www.dfs.ny.gov/reports_and_publications/press_releases/pr1510051 . 
 
3 
The dollar exchange value of cryptocurrencies such as bitcoin is volatile. For example, on April 
7, 2025, one bitcoin was valued at $76,270.13. On October 5, 2025, the same bitcoin was worth 
$124,714.85. On November 21, 2025, the same bitcoin was worth $84,747.80.16 
 
Regulation of Cryptocurrency 
 
The Federal Election Commission (“FEC”) was presented a similar question in 2014.17 Make 
Your Laws, PAC, Inc. requested an opinion of whether it could accept contributions of up to a 
total of $100 in bitcoin as a contribution. The FEC approved the bitcoin contributions in a 
unanimous advisory opinion on May 8, 2014 and concluded that, under the Federal Election 
Campaign Act, a “contribution” is defined to include “any gift, subscription, loan, advance, or 
deposit of money or anything of value made by any person for the purpose of influencing any 
election for Federal office.” The opinion concluded that bitcoins are “money or anything of 
value” within the meaning of the statute. 
 
The FEC opined that such contributions should be considered “in kind” contributions, and that a 
political committee that receives a contribution in bitcoin should value that contribution based on 
the market value of bitcoin at the time the contribution is received. The commissioners were not 
in agreement that the $100 cap is applicable, but the Commission opined that a committee 
treasurer is “responsible for examining all contributions received for evidence of illegality and 
for ascertaining whether contributions received, when aggregated with other contributions from 
the same contributor, exceed the contribution limitations.”18 Further, the FEC determined that 
bitcoin did not have to be deposited into a depository within ten days, indicating that bitcoin was 
more akin to property, such as stocks and bonds, rather than cash.  
 
The FEC further noted that “government agencies, courts, and others are grappling with complex 
questions regarding whether virtual or other private ‘currencies’ are ‘money’ and whether and 
how virtual currency should be subject to law.”19 The IRS has determined that virtual currencies 
should be treated as property, not currency. However, the Financial Crimes Enforcement 
Network treats cryptocurrency as money, stating that it is “a medium of exchange that operates 
like a currency in some environments.”20 But in daily reality, cryptocurrency is little used as 
currency and is overwhelmingly held as an investment.21 
 
16 https://www.coindesk.com/price/ (last visited February 12, 2026). 
17 Fed. Election Comm'n, Advisory Opinion 2014-02 (May 8, 2014), available at http://saos.fec.gov/aodocs/2014-
02.pdf.   
18 Matea Gold, Federal Election Commission Approves Bitcoin Donations to Political Committees, The Washington 
Post, May 8, 2014, https://www.washingtonpost.com/news/post-politics/wp/2014/05/08/federal-electioncommission-approves-bitcoin-donations-to-political-committees/?utm_term=.88c803e212d1 (last visited Feb 12, 
2026). 
19 See Fed. Election Comm'n, Advisory Opinion 2014-02 (May 8, 2014) at footnote 10. 
20 U.S. Dep't of Treas., Application of FinCEN's Regulations to Persons Administering, Exchanging, or Using 
Virtual Currencies, FIN-2013-G001, 1 (March 18, 2013), https://www.fincen.gov/sites/default/files/shared/FIN-
2013-G001.pdf. 
21 https://www.chainalysis.com/blog/bitcoin-market-data-exchanges-trading/ (last vised February 12, 2026). 
 
4 
Most states that have expressly permitted cryptocurrency contributions have not applied limits 
beyond the applicable contribution limit, and only three ban such contributions altogether.22 
Many of these states have offered little or no guidance on the subject, leaving candidates and 
committees to apply existing state laws in these circumstances. 
 
New York Election Law 
 
The definition of contributions in Election Law § 14-100 includes: “any gift, subscription, 
outstanding loan … advance, or deposit of money or any thing of value, made in connection 
with the nomination for election, or election, of any candidate, or made to promote the success or 
defeat of a political party or principle, or of any ballot proposal” (emphasis added).  
 
Election Law § 14-118(2) requires that contributions greater than one hundred dollars must be in 
the form of a check, draft, other instrument, or by credit card. Further, § 14-118(2) requires that 
all cash, checks, drafts or other instruments be deposited in the account of the candidate or 
committee in the designated depository. 
 
A political committee may not accept anonymous contributions. Election Law § 14-128. If a 
committee accepts an anonymous contribution, it must be reported on the appropriate 
contribution schedule and then remitted to the Office of the State Comptroller. 
 
Discussion: 
 
Cryptocurrencies, such as bitcoin, clearly fall within the “any thing of value” portion of the 
definition of “contributions”; as such, a political committee may accept cryptocurrencies as a 
contribution, provided that the contribution is not made anonymously. The next question is 
whether cryptocurrencies should be treated as currency/money, where the requirements of 
Election Law § 14-118(2) would apply, or if it should be treated as an “in kind” contribution.  
 
The Board concludes that cryptocurrencies should be treated as an “in kind” contribution, 
consistent with the FEC’s approach in AO 2014-02 and the approach of most other states that 
have taken up this issue. A committee that receives a contribution of this type should value the 
contribution based on the market value of the bitcoins at the time the contribution is received. 
 
Contributions made in cryptocurrency are subject to the same limits as “in kind” contributions 
made in any other form. In reporting these contributions, the specific cryptocurrency utilized in 
the contribution must be clearly identified, and the receiving committee’s treasurer is responsible 
for determining whether there is evidence of illegality, and whether the contributions received, 
when aggregated with other contributions from the same contributor, exceed applicable 
contribution limits. 
 
22 See California Codes and Regulations 18421.2; see also https://www.thomsonreuters.com/en-us/posts/news-andmedia/crypto-election-contributions/ (nothing Colorado, Iowa, Ohio, Tennessee permit cryptocurrency contributions 
as non-cash contributions, last visited February 12, 2026).  
 
5 
 
A political committee that receives a contribution in bitcoin must value that contribution based 
on the market value of bitcoin at the time the contribution is received. Due to the volatile nature 
of cryptocurrencies, there will likely be a discrepancy of value between when the cryptocurrency 
was received by the committee and when the cryptocurrency was liquidated. 
 
The Board reserves the right to develop and issue additional reporting requirements with respect 
to cryptocurrency contributions beyond those currently utilized for “in kind” contributions and 
held securities, and will issue appropriate guidance to committees accordingly. 
 
The Board further finds that a political committee is permitted to hold said contributions in a 
bitcoin wallet or third-party investment account. While it is true that a bitcoin wallet does not fall 
within the definition of “depository” in § 14-118 (as it is not a financial institution), the Board 
adopts the findings of the FEC in AO 2014-02 stating: “Like securities that a political committee 
may receive into and hold in a brokerage account, bitcoins may be received into and held in a 
bitcoin wallet until the committee liquidates them.” 
 
 
        STATE BOARD OF ELECTIONS
